本测试块涵盖脑底Willis环血管解剖、动脉瘤与区域性脑卒中综合征：Willis动脉环解剖（颈内动脉ICA、大脑前动脉ACA、前交通动脉ACom、大脑中动脉MCA、大脑后动脉PCA、后交通动脉PCom）；囊状/浆果状动脉瘤好发部位与临床压迫（ACom动脉瘤破裂导致前额叶损伤与视交叉受压；PCom动脉瘤压迫同侧动眼神经出现瞳孔扩大对光反射消失及眼肌麻痹；MCA分叉处动脉瘤）；蛛网膜下腔出血（SAH：剧烈劈裂样霹雳头痛Thunderclap headache，腰穿均匀血性脑脊液，尼莫地平预防血管痉挛缺血）；缺血性脑卒中定位诊断：ACA闭塞（对侧下肢瘫痪及感觉减退明显重于上肢面部、无动性发缄、尿失禁）；MCA闭塞（对侧面部与上肢瘫痪感觉丧失重于下肢、优势半球失语、非优势半球空间忽略、对侧同向偏盲）；PCA闭塞（对侧同向偏盲伴黄斑回避，累及胼胝体压部出现失读不伴失写Alexia without agraphia）；分水岭脑梗死（Watershed infarcts：全身低血压灌注不足导致ACA-MCA分水岭梗死出现“桶中人”综合征Man-in-a-barrel双侧近端上肢无力，MCA-PCA分水岭皮层盲盲区）。

### Question 101: Neuroscience: Cerebral Cortex: Part 3 Focus 01 (Cingulate)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Localization of cognitive and emotional functions to specific regions of the cingulate cortex, particularly the anterior cingulate cortex (ACC) and its role in error detection, conflict monitoring, and motivation, and differentiating it from other cortical areas and subcortical structures involved in similar functions. (皮质前扣带回 (ACC) 在错误检测、冲突监控和动机中的定位，以及与其他皮层区域和皮下结构在类似功能上的区分)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 52-year-old male executive presents to the neurology clinic with a 6-month history of progressive personality changes. His wife reports that he has become increasingly impulsive, irritable, and indecisive. He struggles to plan complex projects at work, often getting sidetracked by irrelevant details or making rash decisions without considering consequences. He also exhibits apathy towards previously enjoyed hobbies and has difficulty initiating tasks. Neurological examination reveals intact motor strength, sensation, and cranial nerves. Cognitive testing shows deficits in executive function, specifically in tasks requiring conflict resolution (e.g., Stroop test) and error monitoring (e.g., Wisconsin Card Sorting Test). MRI of the brain reveals a large, slow-growing mass in the medial frontal lobe, predominantly affecting the anterior portion of the cingulate gyrus bilaterally, with sparing of the primary motor cortex and prefrontal cortex proper. Which of the following structures is MOST likely affected by this lesion, leading to the patient's specific constellation of symptoms?

(A) Primary Motor Cortex (Brodmann area 4)
(B) Premotor Cortex (Brodmann area 6)
(C) Anterior Cingulate Cortex (ACC) (Brodmann areas 24/32)
(D) Parietal Association Cortex (Brodmann area 7)
(E) Temporal Lobe (Brodmann areas 37/38)

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 52-year-old male executive. Age suggests potential for degenerative or neoplastic processes. Occupation highlights reliance on executive functions.
- **Symptoms:** Progressive personality changes (impulsivity, irritability, indecisiveness), difficulty planning, distractibility, apathy, difficulty initiating tasks. These point towards deficits in executive function, motivation, and emotional regulation.
- **Neurological Exam:** Intact motor, sensory, cranial nerves. This rules out primary motor/sensory pathway lesions or widespread cortical dysfunction affecting these basic functions.
- **Cognitive Testing:** Deficits in conflict resolution (Stroop test) and error monitoring (WCST). These are hallmark functions associated with the ACC.
- **MRI Findings:** Large mass in the medial frontal lobe, predominantly affecting the *anterior* portion of the *cingulate gyrus bilaterally*, sparing primary motor cortex and prefrontal cortex proper. This localization is crucial. The anterior cingulate cortex (ACC) is located in the medial frontal lobe, anterior to the motor cortex and superior to the corpus callosum, encompassing Brodmann areas 24 (anterior) and 32 (posterior). The sparing of the primary motor cortex (area 4) and prefrontal cortex proper (areas 9, 10, 11, 46, 47) helps narrow down the lesion site.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the structure MOST likely affected, linking the clinical presentation (executive dysfunction, error monitoring deficits) and MRI findings (medial frontal lesion affecting anterior cingulate gyrus).

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Anterior Cingulate Cortex (ACC):** Part of the limbic lobe, located on the medial surface of the frontal lobe, superior to the corpus callosum. Brodmann areas 24 (anterior), 25, 32 (posterior).
- **Function:** Crucial for executive functions, including error detection, conflict monitoring, response selection, motivation, reward anticipation, and emotional regulation. It integrates cognitive and emotional information. Damage leads to apathy, impulsivity, impaired decision-making, difficulty resolving cognitive conflict (e.g., Stroop effect), and impaired error monitoring.
- **Location:** Medial frontal lobe, anterior to the supplementary motor area (SMA) and primary motor cortex, posterior to the orbitofrontal cortex.
- **Blood Supply:** Anterior cerebral artery (ACA) and middle cerebral artery (MCA).
- **Embryology:** Develops from the medial ectoderm of the neural tube, part of the telencephalon.
- **Clinical Correlation:** The patient's symptoms (impulsivity, indecisiveness, apathy, difficulty planning, impaired conflict resolution, impaired error monitoring) directly align with the known functions of the ACC. The MRI findings precisely localize the lesion to this area.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Primary Motor Cortex (Brodmann area 4):** Located on the precentral gyrus of the parietal lobe. Controls voluntary movement. Lesions cause contralateral weakness/paralysis, spasticity, and loss of fine motor control. The patient has intact motor function, and the lesion is medial, not lateral/posterior. This is incorrect.
- (B) **Premotor Cortex (Brodmann area 6):** Located anterior to the primary motor cortex on the lateral surface of the frontal lobe. Involved in planning and sequencing movements, motor learning. Lesions can cause apraxia, difficulty with complex motor tasks, and impaired motor planning, but typically not the profound executive dysfunction and apathy seen here. The lesion is medial, not lateral. This is incorrect.
- (D) **Parietal Association Cortex (Brodmann area 7):** Located in the posterior parietal lobe. Involved in spatial processing, sensory integration, attention, and navigation. Lesions can cause neglect, apraxia, agnosia, and difficulties with spatial orientation. The patient's symptoms are primarily executive and motivational, not spatial or sensory integration deficits. The lesion is medial frontal, not posterior parietal. This is incorrect.
- (E) **Temporal Lobe (Brodmann areas 37/38):** Located inferior to the lateral sulcus. Involved in auditory processing, memory (hippocampus), language comprehension (Wernicke's area), and visual recognition. Lesions can cause auditory hallucinations, memory impairment, language deficits (aphasia), and visual agnosia. The patient's symptoms are executive and motivational, not primarily related to these functions. The lesion is medial frontal, not temporal. This is incorrect.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Structure                     | Location                                  | Primary Function(s)                                  | Key Lesion Symptoms                                                                 | Brodmann Area(s) |
| :--------------------------- | :---------------------------------------- | :--------------------------------------------------- | :---------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | :--------------- |
| **Anterior Cingulate Cortex (ACC)** | Medial Frontal Lobe                      | Error detection, conflict monitoring, motivation, emotional regulation, executive function | Apathy, impulsivity, indecisiveness, impaired conflict resolution, impaired error monitoring | 24, 32           |
| Primary Motor Cortex         | Lateral Surface, Precentral Gyrus         | Voluntary Movement                                   | Contralateral weakness/paralysis, spasticity                                       | 4                |
| Premotor Cortex              | Lateral Surface, Anterior to M1            | Motor planning, sequencing                           | Apraxia, difficulty with complex motor tasks                                       | 6                |
| Parietal Association Cortex   | Posterior Parietal Lobe                   | Spatial processing, sensory integration, attention   | Neglect, apraxia, agnosia, spatial disorientation                                  | 7                |
| Temporal Lobe                | Inferior to Lateral Sulcus                | Auditory processing, memory, language comprehension | Auditory hallucinations, memory impairment, aphasia, visual agnosia                 | 37, 38           |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The anterior cingulate cortex (ACC) is critical for executive functions, particularly error detection and conflict monitoring. Lesions in this area typically manifest as apathy, impulsivity, impaired decision-making, and difficulty resolving cognitive conflict. Differentiating ACC lesions from lesions of the prefrontal cortex (more involved in planning and working memory) or basal ganglia (more involved in motor control and habit formation) requires careful localization and understanding of specific cognitive deficits. The ACC is located medially in the frontal lobe, anterior to the motor cortex.

<a id='question-102'></a>

### Question 102: Neuroscience: Cerebral Cortex: Part 3 Focus 02 (Central)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Localization of function within the central sulcus and surrounding cortical areas, specifically relating to sensory integration and motor control, and differentiating between adjacent gyri and sulci. (中央沟及其周围皮层区域的功能定位，特别是感觉整合和运动控制，以及区分相邻回和裂。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old male presents to the emergency department after experiencing sudden onset of right-sided weakness and numbness, primarily affecting his face and arm, along with difficulty speaking. His symptoms began approximately 45 minutes prior to arrival. Past medical history is significant for hypertension and hyperlipidemia, both poorly controlled. On examination, his blood pressure is 180/105 mmHg. Neurological examination reveals right hemiparesis (face and arm > leg), right hemisensory loss (face and arm > leg), and expressive aphasia (difficulty producing speech, but comprehension is relatively intact). MRI of the brain reveals an acute ischemic infarct in the left hemisphere. The infarct appears to involve the superior portion of the precentral gyrus and the immediately adjacent superior portion of the postcentral gyrus, extending slightly into the underlying white matter. Which cortical area is most likely affected by this infarct, leading to the patient's specific constellation of symptoms?

(A) Primary motor cortex (Brodmann area 4) controlling the leg and foot.
(B) Primary somatosensory cortex (Brodmann area 3b) receiving input from the face and arm.
(C) Premotor cortex (Brodmann area 6) involved in planning movements.
(D) Primary motor cortex (Brodmann area 4) controlling the face and arm.
(E) Primary visual cortex (Brodmann area 17) located in the occipital lobe.

#### Correct Answer: D

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 62-year-old male with sudden onset right-sided weakness (face/arm > leg), right-sided numbness (face/arm > leg), and expressive aphasia. This points to a left hemisphere lesion affecting motor, sensory, and language areas.
- **Vitals/History:** Hypertension and hyperlipidemia are risk factors for stroke. High BP supports acute event.
- **Neurological Exam:** Right hemiparesis (face/arm > leg) indicates damage to the left motor cortex, specifically the area controlling the face and arm, which are located superiorly and laterally within the precentral gyrus. Right hemisensory loss (face/arm > leg) indicates damage to the left somatosensory cortex, specifically the area receiving input from the face and arm, located superiorly and laterally within the postcentral gyrus. Expressive aphasia (Broca's aphasia) typically results from damage to Broca's area, located in the inferior frontal gyrus (part of the frontal lobe), usually in the dominant (left) hemisphere.
- **MRI Findings:** Acute ischemic infarct in the left hemisphere involving the superior portion of the precentral gyrus (motor cortex) and the immediately adjacent superior portion of the postcentral gyrus (sensory cortex), extending slightly into the white matter.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the specific cortical area affected, correlating the clinical findings with the MRI location. The key is the "superior portion" of the precentral and postcentral gyri and the specific motor/sensory deficits (face/arm > leg).

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Precentral Gyrus (Primary Motor Cortex, Brodmann Area 4):** Located in the frontal lobe, anterior to the central sulcus. It controls voluntary movements. The motor homunculus represents the cortical area dedicated to different body parts; the face and upper limb have disproportionately large representations located superiorly and laterally within the gyrus. A lesion affecting the superior portion of the left precentral gyrus would cause weakness primarily in the right face and arm.
- **Postcentral Gyrus (Primary Somatosensory Cortex, Brodmann Area 3b):** Located in the parietal lobe, posterior to the central sulcus. It receives sensory information (touch, pain, temperature, proprioception) from the contralateral body. Like the motor cortex, it has a somatotopic organization (sensory homunculus). The superior portion receives input from the face and arm. A lesion affecting the superior portion of the left postcentral gyrus would cause sensory loss primarily in the right face and arm.
- **Central Sulcus:** The prominent sulcus separating the frontal lobe (precentral gyrus) from the parietal lobe (postcentral gyrus).
- **Aphasia:** Expressive aphasia (Broca's aphasia) is typically caused by lesions in Broca's area (Brodmann areas 44 and 45), located in the inferior frontal gyrus of the dominant hemisphere (usually left). While the vignette mentions expressive aphasia, the MRI findings specifically localize the infarct to the superior portions of the precentral and postcentral gyri. The question asks which *cortical area* is most likely affected *based on the MRI findings*, which point to the superior motor and sensory cortices. The aphasia might be due to extension of the infarct or a separate, smaller lesion, but the primary location described is the superior motor/sensory cortex.
- **Embryology:** The cerebral cortex develops from the neural tube via neurogenesis and neuronal migration. Brodmann areas reflect cytoarchitectural differences. The precentral and postcentral gyri are key landmarks formed during cortical development.
- **Clinical Correlation:** The patient's right face/arm weakness and sensory loss are classic signs of a lesion affecting the superior portions of the left primary motor cortex (precentral gyrus) and primary somatosensory cortex (postcentral gyrus), respectively. The MRI findings confirm this localization.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **Incorrect.** This option refers to the primary motor cortex controlling the leg and foot. This area is located in the *inferior* portion of the precentral gyrus. The patient's symptoms primarily affect the face and arm, not the leg.
- (B): **Incorrect.** This option refers to the primary somatosensory cortex receiving input from the face and arm. While the MRI *does* involve the superior postcentral gyrus (sensory cortex for face/arm), the question asks for the *cortical area* affected, and the infarct involves *both* the superior precentral (motor) and postcentral (sensory) gyri. Option (D) is more comprehensive as it includes the motor cortex, which is also explicitly mentioned in the MRI findings and directly correlates with the motor deficits. However, the superior postcentral gyrus *is* involved. The best answer must encompass the *entire* described lesion based on the clinical presentation (motor and sensory deficits in face/arm) and MRI findings (superior precentral and postcentral gyri). Option (D) focuses on the motor cortex, which is a major component of the lesion and explains the motor deficits. Option (B) focuses solely on the sensory cortex. Given the prominent motor deficits and the involvement of the precentral gyrus, (D) is a better fit, although (B) is partially correct regarding the sensory component. The question asks for *the* cortical area, implying the primary site responsible for the most significant symptoms or the most clearly defined anatomical location in the MRI. The superior motor cortex (face/arm) is a key part of the lesion.
- (C): **Incorrect.** The premotor cortex (Brodmann area 6) is located anterior to the precentral gyrus. It is involved in planning and sequencing movements, not the primary execution (which is area 4). While damage here can cause motor deficits, the primary motor cortex (area 4) is more directly implicated by the location (precentral gyrus) and the specific deficits described.
- (E): **Incorrect.** The primary visual cortex (Brodmann area 17) is located in the occipital lobe and is responsible for processing visual information. A lesion here would cause visual field defects, not hemiparesis, hemisensory loss, or aphasia.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Feature             | Primary Motor Cortex (Area 4) | Primary Somatosensory Cortex (Area 3b) | Premotor Cortex (Area 6) | Primary Visual Cortex (Area 17) |
|----------------------|--------------------------------|---------------------------------------|---------------------------|---------------------------------|
| **Lobe**             | Frontal                        | Parietal                              | Frontal                   | Occipital                       |
| **Location**         | Precentral Gyrus               | Postcentral Gyrus                     | Anterior to Precentral Gyrus | Occipital Lobe                  |
| **Function**         | Voluntary Movement Execution   | Sensory Perception                     | Movement Planning         | Visual Processing               |
| **Lesion Effect**    | Contralateral Weakness/Paralysis | Contralateral Sensory Loss            | Impaired Motor Planning   | Visual Field Deficits           |
| **Homunculus Area**  | Superior = Face/Arm; Inferior = Leg/Trunk | Superior = Face/Arm; Inferior = Leg/Trunk | N/A                       | N/A                             |
| **MRI in Vignette**  | Involved (Superior Portion)    | Involved (Superior Portion)            | Not Mentioned             | Not Mentioned                   |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The superior portion of the precentral gyrus (primary motor cortex, Brodmann area 4) controls movements of the contralateral face and arm, while the superior portion of the postcentral gyrus (primary somatosensory cortex, Brodmann area 3b) receives sensory input from the same regions. Lesions in these areas cause contralateral face/arm deficits. Remember the motor and sensory homunculi and their locations within the respective gyri. Differentiate these from the premotor cortex (anterior to precentral gyrus, movement planning) and other cortical areas like the visual cortex.

<a id='question-103'></a>

### Question 103: Neuroscience: Cerebral Cortex: Part 3 Focus 03 (sulcus)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Localization of neurological deficits to specific cortical areas based on clinical presentation, specifically relating sensory deficits to the postcentral gyrus and its associated sulci, and understanding the embryological origin of cortical structures. (神经系统定位：基于临床表现定位皮层特定区域，特别是感觉障碍与中心后回及其相关沟壑的关系，以及皮层结构的胚胎发生学起源。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old male presents to the emergency department after experiencing sudden onset of right-sided weakness and numbness. He reports that the symptoms began approximately 2 hours ago while he was gardening. His past medical history is significant for hypertension and hyperlipidemia, both poorly controlled. On examination, his vital signs are stable. Neurological examination reveals right hemiparesis (strength 3/5 in the right upper and lower extremities) and right hemisensory loss to light touch and pinprick, predominantly affecting the face, arm, and hand, with relative sparing of the leg. Reflexes are hyperactive on the right side. Cranial nerves are intact. An emergent non-contrast head CT scan shows a left middle cerebral artery (MCA) territory infarct. The patient is being considered for thrombolytic therapy. Given the patient's sensory deficits, which cortical area is most likely affected by the infarct?

(A) Primary motor cortex (Area 4)
(B) Primary visual cortex (Area 17)
(C) Primary auditory cortex (Area 41)
(D) Primary somatosensory cortex (Areas 3, 1, 2)
(E) Broca's area (Area 44)

#### Correct Answer: D

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 62-year-old male with sudden onset right hemiparesis and hemisensory loss (face, arm, hand > leg). This pattern strongly suggests a lesion in the contralateral (left) cerebral hemisphere affecting motor and sensory pathways.
- **Past Medical History:** Hypertension and hyperlipidemia are risk factors for stroke.
- **Neurological Examination:** Right hemiparesis (motor deficit) and right hemisensory loss (sensory deficit) involving the face, arm, and hand, with relative sparing of the leg. This distribution corresponds to the homunculus representation in the motor and sensory cortices. The face, arm, and hand occupy a disproportionately large area of the cortex compared to the leg.
- **Imaging:** Left MCA territory infarct. The MCA supplies the lateral surface of the frontal, parietal, and temporal lobes, including the primary motor cortex, primary somatosensory cortex, and parts of the visual and auditory cortices.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the cortical area most likely affected, given the sensory deficits. The sensory deficits (right hemisensory loss, face/arm/hand > leg) point directly to the primary somatosensory cortex.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Primary Somatosensory Cortex (Areas 3, 1, 2):** Located in the postcentral gyrus of the parietal lobe. It receives sensory information (touch, pain, temperature, proprioception) from the contralateral side of the body. The cortical representation is somatotopically organized (sensory homunculus), with the face, hands, and arms having larger cortical areas than the trunk and legs.
- **Location:** The postcentral gyrus is bounded anteriorly by the central sulcus (separating it from the primary motor cortex in the precentral gyrus) and posteriorly by the parieto-occipital sulcus. Superiorly, it is bounded by the superior parietal lobule, and inferiorly by the lateral sulcus (Sylvian fissure).
- **Blood Supply:** Primarily supplied by the middle cerebral artery (MCA), specifically the superior division. An MCA infarct often affects both the motor and sensory cortices, leading to contralateral hemiparesis and hemisensory loss.
- **Embryology:** Develops from the dorsal part of the neural tube, specifically the marginal zone of the developing cortex.
- **Clinical Correlation:** The patient's right hemisensory loss, particularly affecting the face, arm, and hand more than the leg, is characteristic of a lesion in the left primary somatosensory cortex (postcentral gyrus). The MCA infarct explains the involvement of this area.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Primary motor cortex (Area 4):** Located in the precentral gyrus, anterior to the central sulcus. Responsible for voluntary motor control. While an MCA infarct can affect this area, causing contralateral hemiparesis (which the patient has), the question specifically asks about the cortical area responsible for the *sensory* deficits. The sensory deficits are the primary clue pointing away from the motor cortex.
- (B) **Primary visual cortex (Area 17):** Located in the occipital lobe. Responsible for processing visual information. Lesions here cause visual field defects (e.g., homonymous hemianopia). The patient has no reported visual symptoms, and this area is typically supplied by the posterior cerebral artery (PCA), not the MCA.
- (C) **Primary auditory cortex (Area 41):** Located in the superior temporal gyrus, within the temporal lobe. Responsible for processing auditory information. Lesions here cause auditory processing deficits. This area is supplied by the MCA, but the patient's symptoms are motor and sensory, not auditory.
- (E) **Broca's area (Area 44):** Located in the inferior frontal gyrus of the dominant hemisphere (usually left). Responsible for speech production. Lesions here cause expressive aphasia (Broca's aphasia). The patient has no reported speech difficulties, and while located in the MCA territory, it doesn't explain the sensory deficits.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Structure                     | Location                     | Function                                  | Blood Supply | Key Clinical Deficit                               | Sulci Boundaries                                  |
| :--------------------------- | :--------------------------- | :---------------------------------------- | :----------- | :------------------------------------------------- | :------------------------------------------------ |
| Primary Somatosensory Cortex | Postcentral Gyrus (Parietal) | Sensory perception (touch, pain, temp, etc.) | MCA          | Contralateral hemisensory loss (face/arm/hand > leg) | Central Sulcus (anterior), Parieto-occipital Sulcus (posterior) |
| Primary Motor Cortex         | Precentral Gyrus (Frontal)   | Voluntary motor control                    | MCA          | Contralateral hemiparesis/hemiplegia                | Central Sulcus (posterior)                         |
| Primary Visual Cortex        | Occipital Lobe               | Visual processing                          | PCA          | Homonymous hemianopia                               | Calcarine Sulcus                                  |
| Primary Auditory Cortex      | Temporal Lobe (Superior Gyrus) | Auditory processing                        | MCA          | Auditory agnosia                                    | Lateral Sulcus (inferior), Parieto-occipital Sulcus (posterior) |
| Broca's Area                 | Inferior Frontal Gyrus       | Speech production                          | MCA          | Expressive aphasia                                  | Lateral Sulcus (superior), Superior Frontal Sulcus |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The clinical presentation of contralateral hemisensory loss, particularly with a face/arm/hand predominance, strongly localizes the lesion to the primary somatosensory cortex (postcentral gyrus) in the parietal lobe. Remember the somatotopic organization (sensory homunculus) and the typical blood supply (MCA) for this area. Differentiating this from motor cortex deficits (hemiparesis) is crucial. Visual cortex lesions cause visual field defects, auditory cortex lesions cause hearing deficits, and Broca's area lesions cause expressive aphasia.

<a id='question-104'></a>

### Question 104: Neuroscience: Cerebral Cortex: Part 3 Focus 04 (Postcentral)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Localization of sensory deficits (touch, proprioception, vibration) to the primary somatosensory cortex (postcentral gyrus) and its corresponding thalamic relay (ventral posterolateral nucleus, VPL) following a vascular event. (定位感觉皮层损伤及相关丘脑核)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old male with a history of hypertension and hyperlipidemia presents to the emergency department with acute onset of right-sided sensory loss. The onset was sudden, approximately 30 minutes prior to arrival. He reports numbness and tingling affecting his right face, arm, and hand, but sparing his leg. Neurological examination reveals decreased sensation to light touch, pinprick, vibration, and proprioception on the right side of his body, affecting the face, upper limb, and trunk, with a clear demarcation at the midline. Motor strength, cranial nerve function (except for facial sensation), and reflexes are intact. A non-contrast head CT scan shows a small, acute ischemic infarct in the left parietal lobe.

Which anatomical structure is most likely affected by this infarct?

(A) Left ventral posteromedial nucleus (VPM) of the thalamus
(B) Left primary motor cortex (precentral gyrus)
(C) Left primary somatosensory cortex (postcentral gyrus)
(D) Left superior parietal lobule
(E) Left internal capsule

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 62-year-old male with vascular risk factors (hypertension, hyperlipidemia). This increases the likelihood of a stroke.
- **Onset:** Acute onset (30 minutes) suggests a vascular event (ischemic or hemorrhagic stroke).
- **Symptoms:** Right-sided sensory loss (numbness, tingling) affecting face, arm, and hand, but sparing the leg. This indicates a contralateral sensory deficit.
- **Neurological Exam:** Decreased sensation (light touch, pinprick, vibration, proprioception) on the right side of the body, affecting the face, upper limb, and trunk, with a clear midline demarcation. This pattern corresponds to the sensory homunculus. Motor function is intact, ruling out primary motor cortex involvement.
- **Imaging:** Non-contrast head CT shows an acute ischemic infarct in the left parietal lobe. This confirms a stroke affecting the left hemisphere.
- **Question:** Asks for the specific anatomical structure affected by the infarct, causing the described sensory deficit.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Sensory Pathway:** Sensory information from the right side of the body travels via the spinothalamic tract (pain, temperature) and the dorsal column-medial lemniscus pathway (touch, vibration, proprioception) to the contralateral (left) thalamus.
- **Thalamic Relay:** The spinothalamic tract projects primarily to the ventral posterolateral nucleus (VPL) of the thalamus. The dorsal column-medial lemniscus pathway also projects primarily to the VPL.
- **Cortical Projection:** Neurons in the VPL project via the thalamocortical radiations to the primary somatosensory cortex (S1), located in the postcentral gyrus of the parietal lobe.
- **Somatotopic Organization:** The primary somatosensory cortex has a somatotopic map (sensory homunculus), where different body parts are represented in specific regions. The face, arm, and hand are located in the superior part of the postcentral gyrus, while the trunk and leg are located in the inferior part.
- **Clinical Correlation:** The patient's right-sided sensory loss affecting the face, arm, and hand, but sparing the leg, precisely matches the distribution represented in the superior portion of the left postcentral gyrus. The infarct in the left parietal lobe, specifically affecting this region, explains the clinical presentation. The VPL nucleus relays sensory information from the body, but the question asks for the cortical structure responsible for conscious perception of sensation, which is the postcentral gyrus. The superior parietal lobule is involved in higher-order sensory processing (e.g., spatial awareness, integration), but the primary sensory deficit points to S1. The precentral gyrus is the primary motor cortex. The internal capsule contains ascending sensory fibers, but a lesion here would typically cause more widespread sensory loss, often involving the leg as well, and potentially motor deficits. The VPM nucleus relays sensory information from the face, not the body.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) Left ventral posteromedial nucleus (VPM) of the thalamus: The VPM relays sensory information from the *face* to the primary somatosensory cortex. While the patient has facial sensory loss, the infarct is described as being in the parietal lobe, and the VPM is a thalamic nucleus, not the cortical area directly affected by the infarct causing the cortical sensory deficit. The primary sensory cortex receives input from both VPL (body) and VPM (face). The question asks for the structure *affected by the infarct* causing the sensory loss, and the infarct is in the cortex (parietal lobe).
- (B) Left primary motor cortex (precentral gyrus): This area is responsible for voluntary motor control. The patient has no motor deficits, making this option incorrect. The precentral gyrus is located anterior to the postcentral gyrus, separated by the central sulcus.
- (D) Left superior parietal lobule: This region is involved in higher-order sensory processing, spatial awareness, and integration of sensory information. While located near the postcentral gyrus, a lesion here typically causes deficits like astereognosis, contralateral neglect, or Gerstmann syndrome, rather than the specific somatotopic sensory loss described. The primary sensory deficit points to S1.
- (E) Left internal capsule: This white matter structure contains ascending sensory fibers (from the VPL to the cortex) and descending motor fibers. A lesion here could cause contralateral sensory and motor deficits. However, the infarct is specifically localized to the parietal lobe (postcentral gyrus) on the CT scan, and the sensory loss pattern precisely matches the somatotopic map of the postcentral gyrus, making the cortex itself the most likely site of the lesion causing the symptoms. An internal capsule lesion would likely affect fibers from the leg as well, and potentially motor pathways.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Structure                     | Location                     | Function                                      | Sensory Deficit Pattern (Left Lesion) | Key Association                                  |
| :--------------------------- | :--------------------------- | :-------------------------------------------- | :------------------------------------ | :----------------------------------------------- |
| Postcentral Gyrus (S1)       | Parietal Lobe                | Primary Somatosensory Cortex                  | Contralateral body (face, arm, trunk > leg) | Somatotopic map (sensory homunculus)             |
| Precentral Gyrus (M1)        | Frontal Lobe                 | Primary Motor Cortex                           | Contralateral motor weakness/paralysis | Decussation in medulla (pyramidal tracts)        |
| VPL Thalamus                 | Thalamus                     | Sensory Relay Nucleus (Body)                  | Contralateral body (face, arm, trunk > leg) | Receives input from spinothalamic & DCML tracts |
| VPM Thalamus                 | Thalamus                     | Sensory Relay Nucleus (Face)                  | Contralateral face                    | Receives input from trigeminal lemniscus          |
| Superior Parietal Lobule     | Parietal Lobe (posterior)    | Higher-order sensory processing, spatial awareness | Astereognosis, neglect, Gerstmann's | Integrates sensory information                   |
| Internal Capsule             | Deep White Matter            | Fiber tract (ascending sensory, descending motor) | Variable, often widespread sensory/motor | Common site for lacunar infarcts                 |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The primary somatosensory cortex (postcentral gyrus) receives sensory input from the contralateral side of the body via the thalamus (VPL for body, VPM for face) and processes it according to a somatotopic map (sensory homunculus). Lesions here cause contralateral sensory loss corresponding to the affected region of the cortex. Differentiating S1 from the thalamus (VPL/VPM) requires understanding that the question asks for the cortical structure affected by the *cortical infarct*. Key differentials include the primary motor cortex (precentral gyrus), higher-order association areas (superior parietal lobule), and subcortical white matter tracts (internal capsule).

<a id='question-105'></a>

### Question 105: Neuroscience: Cerebral Cortex: Part 3 Focus 05 (Corpus)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Localization of function within the cerebral cortex, specifically relating damage to the corpus callosum to associated clinical deficits, particularly related to interhemispheric transfer and apraxia. (大脑皮层功能定位，特别是损伤胼胝体与相关临床表现，如半球间转移障碍和失用症的关系)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 52-year-old male presents to the emergency department after experiencing sudden onset of severe headache followed by progressive right-sided weakness and difficulty speaking. He has a history of hypertension and hyperlipidemia. Neurological examination reveals expressive aphasia (Broca's aphasia), right hemiparesis (strength 3/5 in the right upper and lower extremities), and right facial droop. Notably, when asked to copy a drawing of intersecting pentagons (a test of visuospatial constructional ability), he performs poorly with his left hand but relatively well with his right hand. Furthermore, when asked to point to a specific body part (e.g., nose, ear) with his left hand while his eyes are closed, he consistently points to the corresponding part on his right side (left-right disorientation). MRI of the brain reveals a large, acute ischemic stroke affecting the left medial frontal lobe, extending into the anterior portion of the corpus callosum.

Which of the following deficits is MOST likely due to damage to the corpus callosum in this patient?

(A) Right hemiparesis due to damage to the left primary motor cortex.
(B) Expressive aphasia due to damage to Broca's area in the left frontal lobe.
(C) Impaired interhemispheric transfer of tactile information, leading to the inability to identify objects placed in the left hand.
(D) Left-right disorientation with the left hand due to damage to the left parietal lobe.
(E) Visuospatial neglect of the right visual field due to damage to the right parietal lobe.

#### Correct Answer: (C)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 52M with sudden headache, right hemiparesis, expressive aphasia. Suggests a stroke affecting the left hemisphere.
- **Neurological Exam:**
    - Right hemiparesis: Localizes to the left motor cortex or descending pathways (corticospinal tract).
    - Expressive aphasia (Broca's): Localizes to Broca's area in the left inferior frontal gyrus.
    - Poor left-hand performance on intersecting pentagons: Suggests left parietal lobe dysfunction (visuospatial/constructional deficits).
    - Left-right disorientation with left hand: A classic sign of corpus callosum damage, specifically affecting transfer of information from the left hemisphere (where body schema is typically dominant) to the right hemisphere.
- **MRI Findings:** Left medial frontal lobe stroke extending into the anterior corpus callosum. This confirms damage to the left hemisphere structures and the corpus callosum.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the deficit *most likely* due to corpus callosum damage.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Corpus Callosum:** The largest white matter tract in the brain, connecting the left and right cerebral hemispheres. It allows for rapid interhemispheric communication and transfer of information (sensory, motor, cognitive).
- **Anatomy:** The corpus callosum is divided into regions: genu, rostrum, body (corpus), splenium. The anterior portion (genu and rostrum) connects the frontal lobes, while the posterior portion (splenium) connects the occipital and parietal lobes.
- **Function:** Facilitates communication between homologous cortical areas. Damage disrupts this communication.
- **Clinical Manifestations of Callosal Lesions:**
    - **Alien Hand Syndrome:** One hand acts seemingly on its own, often performing complex or inappropriate actions.
    - **Interhemispheric Transfer Deficits:** Difficulty transferring information from one hemisphere to the other. Examples include:
        - Tactile transfer: Inability to identify an object placed in one hand (e.g., left hand) if the patient must name it verbally (language typically lateralized to the left hemisphere).
        - Motor transfer: Difficulty copying a drawing with one hand (often the non-dominant hand) while the other hand observes.
        - Visual transfer: Difficulty describing a picture seen only by one eye (if visual information is processed contralaterally).
    - **Left-Right Disorientation:** Difficulty distinguishing left from right, especially with the non-dominant hand. This is thought to be due to the left hemisphere's dominant role in body schema representation, which is normally transferred to the right hemisphere via the callosum for motor execution.
    - **Apraxia:** Difficulty performing learned motor tasks despite intact motor function and comprehension. Callosal lesions can contribute to apraxia by disrupting interhemispheric coordination.
- **Vignette Correlation:** The patient has a stroke involving the left medial frontal lobe and anterior corpus callosum. The left-right disorientation with the left hand is a classic sign of anterior corpus callosum damage, disrupting the transfer of body schema information from the dominant left hemisphere to the right hemisphere for motor execution. The poor performance on the intersecting pentagons with the left hand also points to impaired interhemispheric transfer.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Right hemiparesis is caused by damage to the left motor cortex (precentral gyrus) or the descending corticospinal tract, *not* the corpus callosum itself. The corpus callosum connects hemispheres, it doesn't directly control motor execution. This is a plausible symptom given the stroke location but is not *due to* the corpus callosum damage.
- (B): Expressive aphasia (Broca's aphasia) is caused by damage to Broca's area, located in the left inferior frontal gyrus. While this area is affected by the stroke, the deficit is not due to corpus callosum damage.
- (D): Left-right disorientation *is* a sign of corpus callosum damage, but the vignette specifically describes it occurring with the *left hand*. While damage to the left parietal lobe can cause various deficits, including contralateral neglect or apraxia, the specific finding of left-right disorientation with the *left hand* is more classically associated with disruption of interhemispheric transfer via the corpus callosum, particularly the anterior portion. The question asks for the deficit *most likely* due to the corpus callosum damage, and tactile/motor transfer deficits like left-right disorientation are hallmark signs.
- (E): Visuospatial neglect is typically associated with damage to the *non-dominant* (usually right) parietal lobe. This patient has a *left* hemisphere stroke. While the left parietal lobe is involved (indicated by poor drawing performance), neglect of the *right* visual field is not the primary deficit described and is not typically caused by left hemisphere lesions or corpus callosum damage.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Feature                 | Corpus Callosum Damage (Anterior) | Left Motor Cortex Damage | Broca's Area Damage | Left Parietal Lobe Damage | Right Parietal Lobe Damage |
| :---------------------- | :-------------------------------- | :----------------------- | :------------------ | :------------------------ | :------------------------ |
| **Primary Deficit**     | Interhemispheric Transfer Deficits, Left-Right Disorientation (esp. left hand) | Contralateral Hemiparesis (Right) | Expressive Aphasia        | Visuospatial/Constructional Deficits, Apraxia | Visuospatial Neglect (Left Field) |
| **Location**            | Connects Frontal Lobes             | Precentral Gyrus        | Inferior Frontal Gyrus | Superior Parietal Lobule   | Superior Parietal Lobule   |
| **Key Clinical Sign**   | Alien Hand, Tactile Transfer Failure, Left-Right Disorientation | Weakness on one side | Difficulty speaking fluently | Difficulty copying drawings | Ignoring one side of space |
| **Vignette Relevance** | Stroke involves anterior CC        | Stroke involves left medial frontal lobe | Stroke involves left medial frontal lobe | Poor drawing performance (left hand) | Not directly relevant |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Damage to the corpus callosum, particularly the anterior portion, disrupts interhemispheric communication, leading to characteristic deficits like impaired tactile transfer, apraxia, and left-right disorientation (often more pronounced with the non-dominant hand). Differentiating these deficits from those caused by direct cortical damage (e.g., motor cortex causing hemiparesis, Broca's area causing aphasia) is crucial for localization. Remember that the corpus callosum connects hemispheres, it does not directly control primary motor or sensory functions or language production.

<a id='question-106'></a>

### Question 106: Neuroscience: Cerebral Cortex: Part 3 Focus 06 (Callosum)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the anatomical organization, embryological development, and clinical consequences of lesions affecting the corpus callosum, specifically relating to interhemispheric transfer of information and potential associated anomalies. (理解胼胝体的解剖组织、胚胎发生以及病变导致的临床后果，特别是半球间信息传递和潜在相关异常。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 32-year-old male presents to the emergency department after a high-speed motor vehicle accident. He is conscious but disoriented, complaining of severe headache and nausea. Neurological examination reveals intact cranial nerves, normal strength and sensation in all extremities, and preserved deep tendon reflexes. However, when asked to name objects presented to his left visual field, he fails to respond verbally, but can point to the object correctly when asked to touch it with his left hand. When asked to read aloud from a book placed in front of him, he reads the words on the right side of the page but cannot read the words on the left side. MRI of the brain reveals a large, acute subdural hematoma overlying the right cerebral hemisphere, causing significant mass effect and midline shift. The imaging also shows an abnormally thin and irregular corpus callosum, particularly in the posterior portion (splenium).

Which of the following embryological defects is MOST likely associated with the observed abnormality of the corpus callosum in this patient?

(A) Failure of the optic vesicles to induce the overlying ectoderm, leading to holoprosencephaly.
(B) Disruption of the midline septum pellucidum formation, resulting in septo-optic dysplasia.
(C) Incomplete fusion of the telencephalic vesicles, leading to agenesis or hypoplasia of the corpus callosum.
(D) Aberrant migration of interneurons from the ganglionic eminence, causing cortical dysplasia.
(E) Defective development of the roof plate, resulting in encephalocele.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 32-year-old male, MVA, headache, nausea, disorientation. Suggests traumatic brain injury (TBI).
- **Neurological Exam:** Intact cranial nerves, strength, sensation, reflexes. Rules out focal motor/sensory deficits typical of stroke or direct cortical injury.
- **Specific Deficits:**
    - **Left visual field neglect/inattention:** Fails to name objects in left visual field (verbal response), but can point to them (non-verbal response). This suggests a disconnection syndrome where visual information from the left visual field (processed by the right hemisphere) cannot be transferred to the language centers (typically left hemisphere) for verbal naming.
    - **Alexia without agraphia:** Cannot read words on the left side of the page (right visual field processed by the left hemisphere), but presumably can write. This also points to a disconnection syndrome where visual information from the right visual field cannot be transferred to the language areas for reading comprehension.
- **Imaging:** Acute subdural hematoma (right hemisphere) causing mass effect and midline shift. This explains the acute neurological symptoms.
- **Anatomical Finding:** Abnormally thin and irregular corpus callosum, especially the splenium. This is a key finding.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the MOST likely embryological defect associated with the observed corpus callosum abnormality.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Corpus Callosum:** The largest white matter tract in the brain, connecting the left and right cerebral hemispheres. It allows for interhemispheric communication and transfer of sensory, motor, and cognitive information.
- **Parts of Corpus Callosum:** Rostrum, genu, body, splenium. The splenium is the most posterior part and connects the occipital lobes.
- **Clinical Syndromes:** Lesions or abnormalities of the corpus callosum (e.g., agenesis, hypoplasia, sectioning) can lead to disconnection syndromes, such as:
    - **Alien hand syndrome:** One hand acts seemingly on its own.
    - **Interhemispheric transfer deficits:** Difficulty naming objects presented to the non-dominant visual field (e.g., left visual field in right-handed individuals), alexia without agraphia (inability to read aloud but ability to write).
    - **Apraxia:** Difficulty performing learned motor tasks.
- **Embryological Development:** The corpus callosum develops from the medial walls of the telencephalic vesicles (primitive cerebral hemispheres) during the 5th-8th weeks of gestation. The axons originating from the hippocampal formation and other cortical areas grow across the midline to form the callosal fibers. Failure of these axons to cross and connect properly, or failure of the structure itself to form completely, results in agenesis or hypoplasia.
- **Association with the Vignette:** The patient's disconnection syndromes (left visual field neglect for naming, alexia without agraphia) are classic signs of impaired interhemispheric transfer, strongly suggesting a corpus callosum abnormality. The MRI confirms this. The question asks for the embryological basis of this abnormality. The corpus callosum develops from the telencephalic vesicles, and its formation involves the growth of axons across the midline. Failure of this process leads to agenesis or hypoplasia.

##### 3. Linking Embryology to the Options
- **Option (C):** Incomplete fusion of the telencephalic vesicles leads directly to agenesis or hypoplasia of the corpus callosum. This fits the clinical presentation and MRI findings perfectly. The corpus callosum is formed by the axons crossing the midline between the two telencephalic vesicles. If this fusion/crossing is incomplete, the structure will be abnormal.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- **(A) Failure of the optic vesicles to induce the overlying ectoderm, leading to holoprosencephaly.** Optic vesicles induce the forebrain. Failure leads to holoprosencephaly (failure of the prosencephalon to divide into two hemispheres), which is a severe developmental anomaly incompatible with the described clinical presentation and MRI findings (intact hemispheres, just an abnormal callosum). The corpus callosum is typically absent or rudimentary in holoprosencephaly, but the primary defect is the failure of hemispheric separation, not specifically the callosum itself.
- **(B) Disruption of the midline septum pellucidum formation, resulting in septo-optic dysplasia.** The septum pellucidum is a thin membrane separating the lateral ventricles. Septo-optic dysplasia (SOD) involves abnormalities of the septum pellucidum, corpus callosum (often hypoplastic or absent), and optic nerve/chiasm. While SOD can involve the corpus callosum, it is a distinct syndrome often associated with optic nerve hypoplasia and pituitary dysfunction. The vignette doesn't mention optic nerve issues or pituitary problems, and the primary finding is the corpus callosum abnormality itself, making (C) a more direct explanation for the isolated callosal defect described. Furthermore, the question asks for the defect *associated* with the observed abnormality, and incomplete telencephalic fusion is the direct cause of callosal agenesis/hypoplasia.
- **(D) Aberrant migration of interneurons from the ganglionic eminence, causing cortical dysplasia.** Cortical dysplasia refers to abnormal development of the cerebral cortex, often involving neuronal migration. This leads to seizures, developmental delay, and focal neurological deficits. It does not directly cause agenesis or hypoplasia of the corpus callosum, although both can co-occur. The patient's symptoms are disconnection syndromes, not typical of cortical dysplasia.
- **(E) Defective development of the roof plate, resulting in encephalocele.** The roof plate is involved in the closure of the neural tube. Defects lead to spina bifida (caudal) or encephalocele/anencephaly (cranial). These are severe neural tube defects, distinct from the development of the corpus callosum within the forebrain vesicles.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Feature             | Corpus Callosum Agenesis/Hypoplasia (Option C) | Holoprosencephaly (Option A) | Septo-Optic Dysplasia (Option B) | Cortical Dysplasia (Option D) | Encephalocele (Option E) |
|----------------------|-------------------------------------------------|------------------------------|---------------------------------|-------------------------------|--------------------------|
| **Primary Defect**   | Incomplete telencephalic fusion/axon crossing  | Failed forebrain division   | Septum pellucidum disruption    | Neuronal migration defect    | Neural tube closure defect|
| **Corpus Callosum**  | Absent or hypoplastic                          | Absent/Rudimentary           | Often absent/hypoplastic         | Usually normal                | Usually normal            |
| **Hemispheres**      | Present, but connected poorly/not at all        | Fused/Partially separated   | Present                         | Present                       | Present (if brain present)|
| **Optic Nerves**     | Normal                                          | Normal                      | Often hypoplastic               | Normal                        | Normal (if brain present)|
| **Clinical Features**| Disconnection syndromes (alexia, naming deficits)| Severe neurological deficits, facial anomalies | Optic nerve issues, pituitary dysfunction, callosal defects | Seizures, developmental delay | Neurological deficits, skull defect |
| **Embryonic Origin**| Telencephalic vesicles                          | Prosencephalon              | Septum pellucidum development   | Cortical development          | Neural tube closure       |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The corpus callosum develops from the fusion of axons crossing the midline between the two telencephalic vesicles. Agenesis or hypoplasia results from incomplete fusion or failure of axonal crossing, leading to disconnection syndromes. Differentiate this from holoprosencephaly (failure of forebrain division), septo-optic dysplasia (involving septum pellucidum and optic nerves), cortical dysplasia (cortical malformation), and encephalocele (neural tube defect).

<a id='question-107'></a>

### Question 107: Neuroscience: Cerebral Cortex: Part 3 Focus 07 (Thalamus)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Localization of thalamic lesions based on clinical presentation, specifically focusing on sensory deficits, motor abnormalities, and cognitive changes related to specific thalamic nuclei and their connections. (基于临床表现定位丘脑损伤，特别是关注与特定丘脑核及其连接相关的感觉、运动和认知变化。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old male presents to the emergency department after experiencing sudden onset of right-sided hemiparesis and sensory loss. He reports difficulty swallowing (dysphagia) and slurred speech (dysarthria). His medical history is significant for hypertension and atrial fibrillation, for which he takes warfarin. Neurological examination reveals right hemiplegia (strength 2/5 in the right upper and lower extremities), right hemisensory loss to light touch and pinprick, and a right homonymous hemianopia. Notably, he exhibits involuntary, large-amplitude, flinging movements of his left arm and leg, particularly when attempting voluntary movements (hemiballismus). Non-contrast head CT scan shows a small, acute lacunar infarct in the left thalamus. Which specific structure within the left thalamus is most likely affected, leading to this constellation of symptoms?

(A) Ventral posterolateral (VPL) nucleus
(B) Ventral posteromedial (VPM) nucleus
(C) Anterior nucleus
(D) Lateral geniculate nucleus (LGN)
(E) Pulvinar nucleus

#### Correct Answer: (A)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 62-year-old male with risk factors (hypertension, atrial fibrillation on warfarin) suggesting potential for embolic stroke.
- **Acute Onset:** Sudden right-sided hemiparesis and sensory loss points towards a vascular event (stroke).
- **Right Hemiplegia & Hemisensory Loss:** Indicates a lesion in the left cerebral hemisphere affecting the motor and sensory pathways.
- **Dysphagia & Dysarthria:** Suggests involvement of cranial nerve nuclei or pathways controlling swallowing and speech, often associated with brainstem or deep hemispheric lesions.
- **Right Homonymous Hemianopia:** Indicates a lesion affecting the left optic radiations or visual cortex.
- **Left Hemiballismus:** Involuntary, violent, flinging movements of the contralateral limbs. This is a key localizing sign.
- **CT Scan:** Confirms an acute lacunar infarct in the left thalamus.
- **Question:** Asks for the specific thalamic structure responsible for the *constellation* of symptoms, particularly the hemiballismus.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Thalamus Overview:** The thalamus is a major relay station for sensory and motor information traveling to the cerebral cortex. It contains numerous nuclei, each with specific connections and functions.
- **Lacunar Infarcts:** Small strokes often affecting deep structures like the thalamus, basal ganglia, pons, and internal capsule, typically due to occlusion of small penetrating arteries.
- **VPL Nucleus:** The Ventral Posterolateral nucleus receives sensory information (pain, temperature, touch, proprioception) from the body via the spinothalamic tract and medial lemniscus. It projects to the primary somatosensory cortex (S1) in the postcentral gyrus. Lesions cause contralateral sensory loss.
- **VPM Nucleus:** The Ventral Posteromedial nucleus receives sensory information (pain, temperature, touch) from the face via the trigeminal lemniscus. It projects to the primary somatosensory cortex (S1) representing the face. Lesions cause contralateral facial sensory loss.
- **Anterior Nucleus:** Part of the limbic system circuit (Papez circuit), receiving input from the mammillary bodies and projecting to the cingulate gyrus. Involved in memory and emotion. Lesions typically cause memory deficits or behavioral changes, not the presented motor/sensory syndrome.
- **Lateral Geniculate Nucleus (LGN):** Relays visual information from the optic tract to the primary visual cortex (V1) in the occipital lobe. Lesions cause contralateral homonymous hemianopia.
- **Pulvinar Nucleus:** The largest thalamic nucleus, involved in visual attention, language processing, and integration of sensory information. Lesions can cause visual field defects, neglect, or cognitive deficits.
- **Hemiballismus:** Characterized by violent, involuntary flinging movements of the limbs, typically contralateral to the lesion. It is classically associated with lesions affecting the subthalamic nucleus (STN), which is located ventral to the thalamus and functionally connected to it. However, thalamic lesions, particularly those involving the VPL nucleus or extending to involve the internal capsule near the STN, can also cause hemiballismus. The VPL nucleus is closely related anatomically to the STN. Damage to the VPL can disrupt the normal inhibitory control exerted by the basal ganglia (via the STN and globus pallidus) on the thalamus, leading to disinhibition and the characteristic ballistic movements. The combination of contralateral sensory loss (VPL), motor deficits (hemiplegia, possibly due to involvement of adjacent internal capsule fibers), visual field defect (possibly involving nearby pathways or connections), and hemiballismus strongly points to a lesion affecting the VPL nucleus or its immediate vicinity. The VPL is the primary sensory relay nucleus for the body, explaining the hemisensory loss. Its proximity to motor pathways and the STN explains the hemiplegia and hemiballismus.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **VPL Nucleus:** This is the correct answer. It receives sensory input from the body and projects to the somatosensory cortex. Lesions cause contralateral sensory loss (present). Its close proximity to the subthalamic nucleus and motor pathways explains the hemiballismus and hemiplegia. The combination of sensory loss and hemiballismus is highly suggestive of VPL involvement or extension.
- (B): **VPM Nucleus:** This nucleus relays sensory information from the face. A lesion here would cause contralateral facial sensory loss, not body sensory loss as described in the vignette. While it's part of the thalamus, it doesn't explain the body sensory deficit or the hemiballismus.
- (C): **Anterior Nucleus:** Involved in memory and emotion (limbic system). Lesions typically cause memory impairment or behavioral changes, not the specific motor and sensory deficits described.
- (D): **Lateral Geniculate Nucleus (LGN):** Relays visual information. A lesion here would cause contralateral homonymous hemianopia (present), but it would not explain the sensory loss, hemiplegia, or hemiballismus.
- (E): **Pulvinar Nucleus:** Involved in visual attention and higher-order sensory integration. Lesions can cause visual field defects, neglect, or cognitive issues, but not typically the specific combination of contralateral sensory loss and hemiballismus seen here.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Structure          | Function                                      | Lesion Symptoms                                                                 | Relation to Vignette |
|--------------------|-----------------------------------------------|---------------------------------------------------------------------------------|----------------------|
| **VPL Nucleus (A)** | Sensory relay (body) to S1                    | Contralateral body sensory loss, hemiballismus (due to proximity to STN/motor fibers) | **Matches**          |
| VPM Nucleus (B)    | Sensory relay (face) to S1                    | Contralateral facial sensory loss                                                | Does not match      |
| Anterior Nucleus (C)| Limbic system (memory, emotion)               | Memory deficits, behavioral changes                                              | Does not match      |
| LGN (D)            | Visual relay to V1                             | Contralateral homonymous hemianopia                                              | Partially matches (hemianopia), but not sensory loss/hemiballismus |
| Pulvinar Nucleus (E)| Higher-order sensory integration, visual attention | Visual field defects, neglect, cognitive deficits                                | Does not match      |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Thalamic lesions can cause a variety of neurological deficits depending on the specific nucleus involved. Hemiballismus is strongly associated with lesions of the subthalamic nucleus, but thalamic lesions, particularly involving the VPL nucleus or its vicinity, can also produce this sign, often accompanied by contralateral sensory loss. Remember the major thalamic nuclei and their primary functions (VPL/VPM for sensory relay, LGN for visual relay, Anterior for limbic function). Differentiating thalamic nuclei lesions requires correlating the specific sensory, motor, or cognitive deficits with the known functions and connections of each nucleus. Key differentials for hemiballismus include STN lesions, thalamic lesions (especially VPL), and rarely, basal ganglia lesions.

<a id='question-108'></a>

### Question 108: Neuroscience: Cerebral Cortex: Part 3 Focus 08 (Splenium)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Localization of neurological deficits to specific regions of the cerebral cortex, specifically the splenium of the corpus callosum, and understanding the functional consequences of lesions in this area. (神经系统定位，特别是胼胝体膝部病变导致的神经功能障碍)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old male with a history of hypertension and hyperlipidemia presents to the emergency department after experiencing sudden onset of severe headache followed by progressive neurological symptoms over 24 hours. He initially complained of difficulty understanding complex sentences and recalling recent events. Over the next day, he developed right-sided hemiparesis, right homonymous hemianopia, and difficulty naming objects (anomia). He also exhibits signs of Gerstmann syndrome on examination, including agraphia (difficulty writing), acalculia (difficulty with calculations), finger agnosia (inability to identify fingers), and left-right disorientation. MRI of the brain reveals a large, acute ischemic infarct involving the posterior aspect of the left occipital lobe and the splenium of the corpus callosum.

Which of the following deficits is MOST likely attributable to the involvement of the splenium of the corpus callosum in this patient?

(A) Right homonymous hemianopia
(B) Right-sided hemiparesis
(C) Gerstmann syndrome
(D) Anomia
(E) Difficulty understanding complex sentences

#### Correct Answer: (C)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 62-year-old male with vascular risk factors (hypertension, hyperlipidemia).
- **Presenting Symptoms:** Sudden headache, difficulty understanding complex sentences, recent memory recall issues, progressive right hemiparesis, right homonymous hemianopia, anomia, Gerstmann syndrome (agraphia, acalculia, finger agnosia, left-right disorientation).
- **Imaging:** MRI shows acute ischemic infarct in the left occipital lobe and the splenium of the corpus callosum.
- **Question Stem:** Asks which deficit is MOST likely due to splenium involvement.

The key clues are the specific deficits associated with the left occipital lobe (right homonymous hemianopia, anomia, difficulty understanding complex sentences) and the left parietal lobe (Gerstmann syndrome), along with the MRI finding of infarction involving both the left occipital lobe and the splenium of the corpus callosum. The question specifically targets the function of the splenium.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Corpus Callosum:** The largest white matter tract in the brain, connecting the left and right cerebral hemispheres. It allows for interhemispheric communication.
- **Splenium:** The posterior-most, thickened part of the corpus callosum. It connects the occipital lobes and the posterior parietal lobes.
- **Function of Splenium:** Primarily involved in transmitting visual information between the hemispheres, integrating visual processing, and potentially contributing to higher-order cognitive functions involving parietal lobe integration.
- **Clinical Correlation:** Lesions of the splenium (e.g., infarct, tumor, trauma) can disrupt interhemispheric transfer of information, particularly visual information. This can manifest as:
    - **Alexia without agraphia:** Inability to read despite intact writing ability, often seen with left occipital lobe lesions extending to the splenium. The right visual cortex can see the words, but the information cannot be transferred to the left language areas (Broca's and Wernicke's areas) for comprehension.
    - **Visual neglect:** Often associated with right parietal lobe lesions, but splenium involvement can contribute.
    - **Disconnection syndromes:** Impaired transfer of sensory or motor information between hemispheres.
    - **Gerstmann syndrome:** Typically associated with lesions in the dominant (usually left) angular gyrus of the parietal lobe. However, the splenium connects the parietal lobes, and damage can potentially contribute to or exacerbate symptoms, especially if the lesion extends into the parietal lobe or disrupts interhemispheric communication relevant to parietal functions. The angular gyrus is involved in language, number processing, spatial cognition, and body image.
- **Other Deficits Explained:**
    - **Right homonymous hemianopia:** Damage to the left visual cortex (occipital lobe) or the optic radiations.
    - **Right-sided hemiparesis:** Damage to the left motor cortex or corticospinal tract.
    - **Anomia:** Damage to the left temporal lobe (Wernicke's area) or parietal lobe (angular gyrus).
    - **Difficulty understanding complex sentences:** Damage to the left temporal lobe (Wernicke's area).
- **Why Gerstmann Syndrome?** While classically linked to the left angular gyrus, the splenium connects the parietal lobes. Disruption of this connection can impair the integrated processing functions associated with the parietal lobes, potentially contributing to the constellation of symptoms seen in Gerstmann syndrome. The infarct involves both the occipital lobe and the splenium, suggesting a larger lesion affecting posterior brain structures. Given the options, Gerstmann syndrome, linked to parietal lobe function, is the most plausible deficit directly related to the splenium's role in interhemispheric parietal communication, especially considering the infarct's location. The other deficits are more clearly attributable to the occipital lobe infarct.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) Right homonymous hemianopia: This is primarily caused by damage to the left primary visual cortex (V1) in the occipital lobe or the optic radiations. While the splenium is posterior, the hemianopia is directly linked to the occipital lobe infarct, not the splenium itself.
- (B) Right-sided hemiparesis: This results from damage to the left motor cortex (precentral gyrus) or the descending corticospinal tract. This is unrelated to the splenium.
- (D) Anomia: This is typically associated with damage to the left temporal lobe (Wernicke's area) or the angular gyrus in the parietal lobe, affecting language processing. While the splenium connects to the parietal lobe, anomia is more directly linked to the occipital/parietal junction or temporal lobe damage in this context.
- (E) Difficulty understanding complex sentences: This is a classic symptom of Wernicke's aphasia, caused by damage to the left posterior superior temporal gyrus (Wernicke's area). This is unrelated to the splenium.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Feature             | Splenium of Corpus Callosum                                  | Occipital Lobe (Left)                                     | Parietal Lobe (Left)                                       | Temporal Lobe (Left)                                      |
|----------------------|--------------------------------------------------------------|-----------------------------------------------------------|-----------------------------------------------------------|-----------------------------------------------------------|
| **Primary Function** | Interhemispheric communication (visual, parietal)             | Visual processing (V1, V2, etc.), visual association areas | Somatosensory integration, spatial awareness, language (angular gyrus) | Auditory processing, language comprehension (Wernicke's), memory |
| **Lesion Deficits**  | Disconnection syndromes (e.g., alexia w/o agraphia), Gerstmann syndrome contribution | Homonymous hemianopia, visual agnosia, alexia w/o agraphia | Gerstmann syndrome, contralateral neglect, apraxia, sensory deficits | Wernicke's aphasia, auditory agnosia, memory deficits      |
| **Relevant Anatomy** | Posterior part of CC, connects occipital & parietal lobes     | V1, V2, V3, V4, V5/MT, calcarine fissure                  | Postcentral gyrus, supramarginal gyrus, angular gyrus      | Superior temporal gyrus, middle temporal gyrus, hippocampus |
| **USMLE Relevance**  | Disconnection syndromes, interhemispheric transfer deficits | Visual pathway, reading disorders                          | Spatial cognition, language, body schema                  | Language comprehension, memory                            |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The splenium of the corpus callosum connects the occipital and parietal lobes. Lesions here can cause disconnection syndromes, particularly affecting visual information transfer (e.g., alexia without agraphia) and potentially contributing to parietal lobe dysfunction like Gerstmann syndrome. Differentiating deficits based on specific white matter tract involvement is crucial for neurological localization. Key differentials for parietal lobe deficits include lesions of the angular gyrus, supramarginal gyrus, or posterior cerebral artery territory.
