<a id='question-149'></a>

### Question 149: Neuroscience: Cerebral Cortex: Part 3 Focus 49 (Brodmann)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Localization of function within the cerebral cortex, specifically relating clinical deficits to damage in Brodmann areas, focusing on the parietal lobe and its association with sensory integration, spatial awareness, and language processing (specifically, angular gyrus). (大脑皮层功能定位，特别是帕里叶皮层损伤导致的临床表现，关注角回的功能。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 68-year-old right-handed woman with a history of hypertension presents to the emergency department after experiencing sudden onset of left-sided weakness and numbness. She also reports difficulty understanding spoken language and trouble reading. Neurological examination reveals right hemiparesis (strength 3/5 in the left upper and lower extremities), left hemisensory loss, and impaired comprehension of complex sentences (Wernicke's aphasia). She also exhibits alexia (inability to read) and agraphia (inability to write), despite intact vision and basic language production. MRI of the brain shows an acute ischemic infarct in the left parietal lobe, specifically involving the angular gyrus and surrounding regions. Which Brodmann area is most likely affected by this infarct, leading to the observed constellation of symptoms?

(A) Brodmann area 4
(B) Brodmann area 17
(C) Brodmann area 22
(D) Brodmann area 39
(E) Brodmann area 40

#### Correct Answer: (E)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 68-year-old right-handed woman, sudden onset left-sided weakness/numbness, difficulty understanding spoken language (Wernicke's aphasia), difficulty reading (alexia), difficulty writing (agraphia). History of hypertension (risk factor for stroke).
- **Neurological Exam:** Right hemiparesis, left hemisensory loss, Wernicke's aphasia, alexia, agraphia.
- **Imaging:** Acute ischemic infarct in the left parietal lobe, involving the angular gyrus.
- **Key Clues:** Left parietal lobe infarct, angular gyrus involvement, combination of sensory deficits, motor deficits (hemiparesis), receptive aphasia (Wernicke's), alexia, and agraphia. The question asks for the Brodmann area involved.
- **Deduction:** The infarct is in the left parietal lobe, affecting the angular gyrus. The symptoms point to deficits in sensory integration, spatial processing, language comprehension, reading, and writing. We need to identify the Brodmann area corresponding to the angular gyrus and associated with these functions.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Brodmann Areas:** Brodmann areas are cytoarchitecturally defined regions of the cerebral cortex.
- **Parietal Lobe:** The parietal lobe is involved in processing sensory information (somatosensory cortex), spatial awareness, navigation, and integrating sensory input.
- **Angular Gyrus:** Located in the posterior part of the superior temporal gyrus and the parietal lobe, at the junction of the temporal, parietal, and occipital lobes. It is crucial for language processing (reading, writing, comprehension), number processing, spatial cognition, and memory retrieval.
- **Brodmann Area 40:** Corresponds largely to the angular gyrus. It is involved in language processing, particularly reading (alexia) and writing (agraphia), as well as integrating visual, auditory, and somatosensory information. Lesions here can cause Gerstmann syndrome (acalculia, agraphia, finger agnosia, left-right disorientation) and alexia without agraphia (if the lesion spares the writing area). The patient's alexia and agraphia strongly implicate Brodmann area 40.
- **Brodmann Area 4:** Primary motor cortex, located in the precentral gyrus. Lesions cause contralateral motor deficits (hemiparesis), but not typically aphasia, alexia, or agraphia.
- **Brodmann Area 17:** Primary visual cortex, located in the occipital lobe. Lesions cause visual field defects (hemianopia), not motor or language deficits.
- **Brodmann Area 22:** Wernicke's area, located in the posterior superior temporal gyrus. Primarily involved in language comprehension. Lesions cause receptive aphasia (Wernicke's aphasia), which the patient has, but Wernicke's area is typically considered part of Brodmann area 22, not the angular gyrus (Brodmann 40). While Wernicke's aphasia is present, the combination with alexia and agraphia points more specifically to the angular gyrus.
- **Brodmann Area 39:** Located in the anterior part of the angular gyrus, often considered part of Wernicke's area or involved in language processing, particularly semantic processing. While related to language, Brodmann area 40 is more specifically associated with the integration functions leading to alexia and agraphia.

##### 3. Embryological Considerations (胚胎发生考点)
- The cerebral cortex develops from the neural tube via neurogenesis and neuronal migration. Brodmann areas reflect differences in cellular structure and organization that arise during this development. The parietal lobe, including the angular gyrus (Brodmann 40), develops from the dorsal part of the telencephalic vesicle. Specific developmental genes and signaling pathways influence the formation and differentiation of these cortical areas.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) Brodmann area 4: Primary motor cortex. Located in the precentral gyrus of the frontal lobe. Lesions cause contralateral weakness (hemiparesis), but not aphasia, alexia, or agraphia. The patient has hemiparesis, but the other symptoms point away from this area being the primary site responsible for the full clinical picture.
- (B) Brodmann area 17: Primary visual cortex. Located in the occipital lobe. Lesions cause visual field deficits (e.g., homonymous hemianopia). The patient has no reported visual symptoms.
- (C) Brodmann area 22: Wernicke's area. Located in the posterior superior temporal gyrus. Lesions cause receptive aphasia (difficulty understanding language). The patient has Wernicke's aphasia, which is consistent with damage to this area. However, the infarct location is specified as the angular gyrus in the parietal lobe, and the presence of alexia and agraphia strongly implicates Brodmann area 40, which is adjacent to and functionally related to Wernicke's area but distinct. While Brodmann 22 contributes to the aphasia, the full constellation of symptoms, especially alexia and agraphia linked to the angular gyrus, points more directly to Brodmann 40.
- (D) Brodmann area 39: Located in the anterior part of the angular gyrus, often associated with Wernicke's area and semantic processing. While involved in language, it is less specifically associated with the combination of alexia and agraphia than Brodmann area 40, which encompasses the posterior part of the angular gyrus.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Feature             | Brodmann Area 4 (Motor Cortex) | Brodmann Area 17 (Visual Cortex) | Brodmann Area 22 (Wernicke's Area) | Brodmann Area 39 (Anterior Angular Gyrus) | Brodmann Area 40 (Angular Gyrus) |
|----------------------|---------------------------------|-----------------------------------|--------------------------------------|-------------------------------------------|-----------------------------------|
| **Location**         | Precentral Gyrus (Frontal Lobe) | Occipital Lobe                    | Posterior Superior Temporal Gyrus    | Anterior Angular Gyrus (Parietal/Temporal) | Angular Gyrus (Parietal/Temporal)  |
| **Primary Function** | Motor Control                   | Visual Processing                 | Language Comprehension               | Language Processing (Semantic)            | Language (Reading/Writing), Integration |
| **Lesion Symptoms**  | Contralateral Weakness          | Visual Field Defects              | Receptive Aphasia                    | Language Deficits                        | Alexia, Agraphia, Gerstmann's      |
| **Patient Symptoms** | Hemiparesis                     | N/A                               | Receptive Aphasia                    | N/A                                       | Alexia, Agraphia, Receptive Aphasia |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The angular gyrus (Brodmann area 40) is a critical hub for integrating sensory information, particularly visual, auditory, and somatosensory input, and is essential for language functions like reading and writing. Lesions here can cause alexia, agraphia, and contribute to receptive aphasia. Differentiating Brodmann areas based on clinical syndromes is crucial. Remember Brodmann 4 (motor), 17 (vision), 22 (Wernicke's/comprehension), and 40 (angular gyrus/reading/writing).

<a id='question-150'></a>

### Question 150: Neuroscience: Cerebral Cortex: Part 3 Focus 50 (numbers)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Localization of function within the cerebral cortex, specifically relating to number processing (acalculia) and associated parietal lobe structures, including the angular gyrus and its connections. (大脑皮层功能定位，特别是数字处理（失算症）和相关的顶叶结构，包括角回及其连接。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old right-handed male presents to the emergency department after experiencing sudden onset of severe headache followed by progressive neurological deficits over the past 24 hours. He has a history of hypertension and hyperlipidemia, managed with lisinopril and atorvastatin. On examination, he is alert but disoriented to time and place. He exhibits right-sided hemiparesis (strength 3/5 in the right upper and lower extremities) and right-sided sensory loss (light touch, pain, temperature, proprioception). Cranial nerves are intact. He demonstrates significant difficulty with basic arithmetic, such as serial subtraction (e.g., 100 - 7 = 93, then 93 - 7 = 86, then 86 - 7 = 79), and cannot correctly identify the number of fingers held up on his left hand. He can, however, write numbers and recognize them when presented individually. An emergent non-contrast head CT scan reveals a large left parieto-occipital hemorrhage with significant mass effect and midline shift. Which of the following structures is MOST likely damaged, leading to his specific deficit in number processing?

(A) Broca's area
(B) Wernicke's area
(C) Angular gyrus
(D) Primary motor cortex
(E) Corpus callosum

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 62-year-old male with sudden onset headache and progressive neurological deficits (suggesting stroke or hemorrhage). History of hypertension (risk factor).
- **Neurological Exam:** Right hemiparesis and sensory loss indicate a lesion in the left cerebral hemisphere affecting motor and sensory pathways. Disorientation suggests involvement of higher cortical functions.
- **Specific Deficit:** Difficulty with serial subtraction and inability to identify the number of fingers (acalculia, specifically dyscalculia involving number comprehension and calculation) while retaining the ability to write and recognize individual numbers. This points towards a specific cognitive function deficit.
- **Imaging:** Left parieto-occipital hemorrhage confirms a lesion in the left hemisphere, consistent with the right-sided motor/sensory deficits. The location (parieto-occipital) is crucial.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the structure MOST likely damaged causing the *specific deficit in number processing*.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Acalculia:** Difficulty with mathematical calculations. Different types exist:
    - **Gerstmann syndrome:** A tetrad associated with damage to the *left angular gyrus* (often due to stroke or trauma), including acalculia, agraphia (difficulty writing), finger agnosia (inability to identify fingers), and left-right disorientation.
    - **Dyscalculia:** Broader term for difficulty with math. Can involve deficits in number comprehension, calculation, or mathematical concepts.
- **Angular Gyrus:** Located in the parietal lobe, at the junction of the temporal and occipital lobes. It plays a crucial role in number processing, language comprehension (reading, writing), spatial cognition, and attention. Damage here classically leads to Gerstmann syndrome, which includes the specific type of acalculia described (difficulty with calculation, especially serial subtraction, and finger agnosia). The patient's inability to identify the number of fingers is a key feature of finger agnosia, strongly implicating the angular gyrus. The hemorrhage location (left parieto-occipital) is consistent with damage to this structure.
- **Broca's Area (Inferior Frontal Gyrus):** Primarily involved in speech production. Damage causes expressive aphasia (difficulty speaking fluently). Not directly related to number processing.
- **Wernicke's Area (Superior Temporal Gyrus):** Primarily involved in language comprehension. Damage causes receptive aphasia (difficulty understanding language). Not directly related to number processing.
- **Primary Motor Cortex (Precentral Gyrus):** Controls voluntary movement. Damage causes contralateral hemiparesis (which the patient has), but not acalculia.
- **Corpus Callosum:** Connects the two cerebral hemispheres. Damage can cause disconnection syndromes, but not typically isolated acalculia or Gerstmann syndrome.

##### 3. Embryological Origin:** The cerebral cortex, including the angular gyrus, develops from the neural tube via the proliferation of neural stem cells in the ventricular zone, followed by migration of neurons to form the six cortical layers. The parietal lobe, where the angular gyrus resides, arises from the dorsal portion of the telencephalic vesicle.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Broca's area:** Incorrect. Located in the inferior frontal gyrus (Brodmann area 44/45). Damage causes expressive aphasia (difficulty producing speech), not acalculia. The patient's primary deficit is number processing, not speech production.
- (B) **Wernicke's area:** Incorrect. Located in the posterior superior temporal gyrus (Brodmann area 22). Damage causes receptive aphasia (difficulty understanding language), not acalculia. The patient's primary deficit is number processing, not language comprehension.
- (D) **Primary motor cortex:** Incorrect. Located in the precentral gyrus. Damage causes contralateral motor deficits (hemiparesis), which the patient *does* have, but it does not explain the specific cognitive deficit of acalculia and finger agnosia. The question asks for the structure responsible for the *number processing* deficit.
- (E) **Corpus callosum:** Incorrect. A large white matter tract connecting the cerebral hemispheres. Damage can lead to disconnection syndromes (e.g., alien hand syndrome), but not typically isolated acalculia or Gerstmann syndrome. The patient's symptoms are localized to a specific cognitive function (number processing) and associated deficits (finger agnosia), pointing to a cortical lesion rather than a major white matter tract disconnection.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Structure             | Location                      | Primary Function(s)                                  | Associated Deficit(s)                                     | Relevance to Case                                                                 |
|----------------------|-------------------------------|------------------------------------------------------|-----------------------------------------------------------|-----------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| **Angular Gyrus (C)** | Left Parietal Lobe            | Number processing, language (reading/writing), spatial cognition | Acalculia, agraphia, finger agnosia, left-right disorientation (Gerstmann syndrome) | **MOST LIKELY** cause of patient's acalculia and finger agnosia. Location fits hemorrhage. |
| Broca's Area (A)     | Left Inferior Frontal Gyrus   | Speech production                                     | Expressive aphasia                                         | Incorrect. Patient has no primary speech production deficit.                         |
| Wernicke's Area (B)  | Left Superior Temporal Gyrus | Language comprehension                               | Receptive aphasia                                         | Incorrect. Patient has no primary language comprehension deficit.                   |
| Motor Cortex (D)     | Precentral Gyrus              | Voluntary movement                                    | Contralateral hemiparesis                                  | Patient has this, but it doesn't explain the acalculia.                             |
| Corpus Callosum (E)  | Interhemispheric              | Interhemispheric communication                        | Disconnection syndromes (e.g., alien hand)                  | Incorrect. Doesn't explain the specific cognitive deficits.                         |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The left angular gyrus is critical for number processing and calculation. Damage to this area, often due to left middle cerebral artery (MCA) stroke or hemorrhage affecting the parietal lobe, can cause acalculia, finger agnosia, agraphia, and left-right disorientation (Gerstmann syndrome). Differentiating between cortical areas based on specific cognitive deficits is crucial for neurological localization. Key differentials for cognitive deficits include Broca's area (speech production), Wernicke's area (language comprehension), and the motor cortex (movement).
